I suspect that the first step for many of them is to panic less about short timelines (e.g. by taking </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LP6uCXs6Ea5qSbWpY/twenty-years-from-rsi-to-takeoff-slow-learning-scaling" rel="noreferrer"><span>scenarios like this one</span></a><span> more seriously)—though I also expect cultivating courage and integrity to make your work dramatically more valuable fairly quickly (</span><a href="https://x.com/richardmcngo/status/2088758525426372848?s=46" rel="noreferrer"><span>as this tweet discusses</span></a><span>). In the longer term, I want the community as a whole to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/fLRPeXihRaiRo5dyX/the-magnitude-of-his-own-folly"><span>halt, melt, and catch fire</span></a><span>: to “Say, ‘I'm not ready.’ Say, ‘I don't know how to do this yet.’” Eliezer’s </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/j9Q8bRmwCgXRYAgcJ/miri-announces-new-death-with-dignity-strategy"><span>Death with Dignity</span></a><span> post was a step towards this, but focused too much on whether we were on track to solve the alignment problem, and too little on the adversarial dynamics that have been pushing us in the wrong direction. I hope that this sequence will point people more directly towards reevaluating.</span></p><p><span>I’ll talk more about my alternative mission of high-integrity scientific research (and why it captures the parts of the field I most want to promote) in my next few posts. For now, I’ll focus on a few high-level principles for starting to move in that direction. The first: on an intuitive level, you should think of many arguments about differential impact on the margin as analogous to arguments for timing a stock market bubble. If someone argues that the market as a whole is in a bubble, but that they’ll invest your money while it’s still going up and sell before it drops, you should probably be very skeptical. I think this analogy is actually quite deep, because the core difficulty in both cases is accounting for other people making decisions which are tightly entangled with yours. It seems possible to account for this in principle, but in practice it’s very easy to fool yourself (especially when you’re used to doing econ-style reasoning about marginal effects)—and when you do so, you’re making the bubble bigger. So I don’t trust myself (or basically anyone else in the field) to think clearly about such cases; it seems far better to focus on more robust strategies.</span></p><p><span>Okay, but how should you evaluate which strategies are robust? One foundational step is to assume that you are choosing on behalf of a significantly wider range of people than just yourself. A range of different considerations support this conclusion, including:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>The idea that you’re setting norms for the field, which helps build a high-trust community.</span></li><li value="2"><span>The idea that others will copy your behavior—whether due to trusting your decision-making process, or simply because you’ve made it more socially permissible for others to behave similarly.</span></li><li value="3"><span>The idea that it’s more important to avoid underestimating than avoid overestimating your influence—because if you underestimate your influence then your actions matter much more than you thought.</span></li><li value="4"><span>The idea that being right about a problem (e.g. AGI risk) is correlated with being right about other things, and so your work might be much more impactful than others’ work.</span></li><li value="5"><span>The idea that others will make decisions which are </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/w/guide-to-logical-decision-theory"><span>logically correlated</span></a><span> with yours.</span></li><li value="6"><span>Underlying deontological or Kantian moral intuitions about universalizability.</span></li></ul><p><span>Someone who followed this principle would be much less likely to join (or stay at) unethical organizations to do “harm mitigation”; and they’d be much less likely to justify racing “because we’re the good guys”. They would also favor research directions that they think would reward deep investigation, rather than shallower ones which mainly seem helpful on the margin (or which are even harmful if too many people pursue them). Deciding how to apply this principle will always require individual judgement, but I’d suggest erring towards overapplying it rather than underapplying it. Even if you thereby leave some value on the table, you’re also helping establish yourself as a more trustworthy person.</span></p><p><span>However, this principle is still quite blunt—especially for people who are in fairly unique situations. A second principle is that, when making more complicated decisions, people should articulate cruxes for their decisions, and then be expected to either acknowledge when those beliefs were disproved, or else clearly publicly state when they’ve changed their cruxes. I think these are much more valuable when done by individuals voicing their own opinions; group statements tend to produce </span><a href="https://aworkinglibrary.com/writing/accountability-sinks"><span>accountability sinks</span></a><span>. For example, if Dario had publicly discussed his intention for Anthropic not to advance capabilities, then it would have been much easier for the alignment community (and Anthropic employees) to respond appropriately when he started pushing the frontier. As it is, not a single Anthropic employee has publicly resigned over this dramatic change in Anthropic’s strategy, which suggests significant frog-boiling dynamics.</span></p><p><span>An example from this sequence is my argument that research is robustly valuable insofar as it a) aims towards a deep scientific understanding, and b) is done by high-integrity people. In the short term, you might disagree that this is a good target; in the longer term, though, seeing me stick to this standard (or explain why I changed it) should help you trust that I’m not being corrupted in the standard ways. Relatedly, I give Paul some credit for writing </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/vwu4kegAEZTBtpT6p/thoughts-on-the-impact-of-rlhf-research"><span>his retrospective on RLHF</span></a><span>, but the arguments still seem very defensive, rather than an attempt at a neutral evaluation of what he did right and wrong. The closest Geoffrey has come to giving such a retrospective is </span><a href="https://www.aisi.gov.uk/blog/why-i-joined-aisi---geoffrey-irving"><span>this post on why he joined AISI</span></a><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnc3dj2amao"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnc3dj2amao">[13]</a></sup></span><span> Almost none of the others who have had most influence over the field (like Yudkowsky, Vassar, Shulman, and Karnofsky) have done so either; I hope that this sequence spurs some of them to do so. I’ll also have a lot more to say about my own mistakes over the next two posts; if you ever think I’m holding other people to a higher standard than I hold myself to, please tell me so.</span></p><p><span>A third standard is that improving the world requires enough integrity to sometimes move away from money, prestige, or power. For example, MIRI was willing to make their research </span><a href="https://intelligence.org/2018/11/22/2018-update-our-new-research-directions/"><span>nondisclosed-by-default</span></a><span> due to concerns about capabilities externalities. Similarly, </span><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/1664992337615900672?s=46"><span>Janus</span></a><span> was </span><a href="https://x.com/repligate/status/1633465636352565248?s=46"><span>aware</span></a><span> of chain-of-thought prompting over a year before </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2201.11903"><span>it became mainstream</span></a><span>; my understanding is that she didn’t publicize it widely due to concerns about accelerating capabilities. It’s notable that it’s precisely the outsiders with fewest resources who are willing to make these sacrifices—contrast Dario being unwilling to hold back even a paper as directly acceleratory as “</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2001.08361"><span>scaling laws</span></a><span>”. (I do somewhat credit Paul and Geoffrey for stepping away from AGI companies to work in government, but not a huge amount, because this still involves moving away from one kind of power towards a different type of power.)</span></p><p><span>To be clear, I’m not against people who care about alignment accruing significant power. Rather, I’m against them doing so under false pretenses, and without possessing a concomitant level of integrity. One reason the alignment community (especially the EA components of it) often fails to track the latter is that it takes charitable donations or altruistically-motivated sacrifices (like veganism) as evidence that people should be trusted. Unfortunately, it turns out that altruism and integrity are two very different things (as SBF showed in dramatic fashion). Much stronger evidence for integrity comes from criticizing or standing up to powerful people even when few others around you are doing so. Unfortunately, there are few clear examples—the main ones are Daniel Kokotajlo at OpenAI, </span><a href="https://time.com/6266923/ai-eliezer-yudkowsky-open-letter-not-enough/"><span>Yudkowsky’s Time essay</span></a><span>, Pause/Stop AI advocacy, and to some extent the OpenAI board and Anthropic’s stand against the Trump administration. I’ll explore these examples in later posts; in my next post, though, I’ll discuss the underlying mindset that “someone else will do it”, which skews many decisions made across the field.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnuv7y7ousjs"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefuv7y7ousjs">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Anyone who read an early draft of this post should note that this public version is over twice as long, and makes a much more detailed and hopefully clearer argument than the original.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnhs7vvol8aj"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefhs7vvol8aj">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This is different from Dan Hendrycks' concept of "</span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/s/FaEBwhhe3otzYKGQt" rel="noreferrer"><span>pragmatic AI safety</span></a><span>". I've appropriated the use of the word "pragmatic", with apologies to Dan, because it seems like his term has fallen out of use. I don't have a strong opinion on how much Dan's research program overlaps with the thing I'm calling "pragmatic alignment".</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnzrauvev4qz"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefzrauvev4qz">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I haven't included direct quotes in the main text because both authors make this point in ways that are only partly true. In </span><i><span>The Infinity Machine </span></i><span>(Page 287), Mallaby writes that “paradoxically, the aggressive scaling favored by Amodei, Irving, and Christiano turned out to be the starting gun in a destabilizing AI race". He was referring to the scaling up of GPT-2, which Paul Christiano tells me he didn't support.</span></p><p><span>Meanwhile, in </span><i><span>Empire of AI</span></i><span>, Karen Hao writes:</span></p><blockquote><p><span>"What is AGI? What does AGI look like?" Amodei said. "Well, you know, we're in the awkward position of, we don't know what it looks like. We don't know when it's going to happen. So we look for things that aren't AGI but that present at least some of the opportunities and difficulties of AGI. And the hope is that if we can handle those things well, then we're kind of, like, ready for the bigger leagues."</span><br /><br /><span>It was a logic that worked under a specific assumption: that AGI, despite being amorphous and unknowable, was also inevitable. OpenAI would repeatedly justify its behaviors against variations of the same argument for years after. Under the specter of AGI's unstoppable arrival, the company needed to keep developing more and more powerful models to prepare itself and to prepare society. Even if those models carried with them their own risks, the experience they offered to prevent or face possible AI apocalypse made those risks bearable.</span><br /><br /><span>[However] it was specifically OpenAI, with its billionaire origins, unique ideological bent, and Altman's singular drive, network, and fundraising talent, that created a ripe combination for its particular vision to emerge and take over.... In other words, everything OpenAI did was the opposite of inevitable; the explosive global costs of its massive deep learning models, and the perilous race it sparked across the industry to scale such models to planetary limits, could only ever have arisen from the one place it actually did."</span></p></blockquote><p><span>I think Hao is incorrect that LLMs could only ever have arisen from OpenAI, because she's not taking Moore's law seriously enough. More generally, her book seems to often be trying to "score points" against the tech industry.</span></p><p><span>Despite this, the fact that both of these authors homed in on AI safety arguments backfiring at AGI companies seems very notable to me.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnmozm8nwqiln"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefmozm8nwqiln">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Because strict adherence to these norms worked out so badly, I partially set them aside in this post; I’m still trying to be fair to everyone involved, but I don’t take people’s stated motivations as authoritative to the extent that rationalists usually do. Instead, I try to build up a better understanding of how sycophancy and fear warped people’s thinking (including my own).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fniblhf4mdsjh"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefiblhf4mdsjh">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>While this is bad for the world, it’s also one of the key reasons that the alignment community is able to exert such outsized influence, as I'll detail in my next post.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnrxtm1tk5u3j"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefrxtm1tk5u3j">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>It's important to note both that this view was "directionally correct" in predicting that AI progress would be faster than almost anyone thought, and also "literally wrong" in that Dario and Jan (and I think Shane) expected that we'd already have AGI by now.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn30md34pqjij"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref30md34pqjij">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>It seems like the root of this conceptual mistake might have come from Paul treating "build AGI" and "align AGI" as sequential steps. In practice, though, we should expect alignment techniques to be applied throughout the process of "building" the AGI. If both the building process and the alignment process are prosaic (or both non-prosaic) then we still have a clean distinction. But if the alignment techniques are non-prosaic, then applying them to an otherwise prosaic AGI creates an edge case in the framework.</span></p><p><span>My guess is that Paul didn't explicitly consider this possibility, because he characterizes the following as an objection to prosaic AI alignment: "Some researchers (especially at MIRI) believe that aligning prosaic AGI is probably infeasible — that the most likely approach to building an aligned AI is to understand intelligence in a much deeper way than we currently do, and that if we manage to build AGI before achieving such an understanding then we are in deep trouble." Whereas this is consistent with non-prosaic alignment techniques being necessary for aligning otherwise-prosaic AIs.</span></p><p><span>This confusion has propagated in part because "prosaic AI alignment" was an extremely poor choice of terminology. Paul seems to have intended it as "[prosaic AI] alignment", but of course it can easily be read as "prosaic [AI alignment]".</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn3a7eec9eo6e"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref3a7eec9eo6e">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>To be clear, I personally (and most alignment researchers at OpenAI) didn't do any better than Paul; I single him out because he was the most influential. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/YiRsCfkJ2ERGpRpen/leogao-s-shortform?commentId=mSupLTMqcZ8Mc8dtZ" rel="noreferrer"><span>Leo Gao</span></a><span> is one of the few people who's now doing a better job.</span></p><p><span>You can read </span><a href="https://sideways-view.com/2016/11/14/integrity-for-consequentialists/" rel="noreferrer"><span>Paul's articulation of his view of integrity here</span></a><span>. Note that while his conclusions are reasonable for interactions between individuals, it seems harder to use such arguments to accurately evaluate the kind of proactive public honesty that would have made a big difference over the last decade. In particular, consider his criterion of "I pretend that picking [an option] causes everyone to know that I am the kind of person who picks that option". This might lead someone to think their options are "A: I'm the kind of person who doesn't critique my employer, and everyone knows that. B: I'm the kind of person who critiques my employer, and everyone knows that." But the biggest problems come when you're the kind of person who doesn't critique your employer, and people trust your employer because they </span><i><span>don't </span></i><span>know that.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn4beiqaqhy6"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref4beiqaqhy6">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Debate is the only </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/DGt9mJNKcfqiesYFZ/debate-oracles-and-obfuscated-arguments-3"><span>approach</span></a><span> to </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/NdovveRcyfxgMoujf/p/iELyAqizJkizBQbfr"><span>scalable</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/NdovveRcyfxgMoujf/p/EgRJtwQurNzz8CEfJ"><span>oversight</span></a><span> with </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/NdovveRcyfxgMoujf/p/CuneN5HmLnztsLRzD"><span>substantive</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/NdovveRcyfxgMoujf/p/8XHBaugB5S3r27MG9"><span>theoretical</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/s/NdovveRcyfxgMoujf/p/XycoFucvxAPhcgJQa"><span>results</span></a><span> (by default I’m counting Paul’s </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ztokaf9harKTmRcn4/a-bird-s-eye-view-of-arc-s-research"><span>current</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/XdQd9gELHakd5pzJA/arc-progress-update-competing-with-sampling"><span>heuristic</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/fsG4m6sRMpomd7Rk6/mechanistic-estimation-for-wide-random-mlps"><span>arguments</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/7RyAefESvb6BQ3tMz/mechanistic-estimation-for-expectations-of-random-products"><span>research</span></a><span> as a different line of work, though I’m open to the idea that there’s something important there which grew out of iterated amplification). I haven’t yet tried to evaluate Geoffrey’s complexity-theoretic approach to analysing debate; however, nothing I’ve seen so far pattern-matches to me as a significant insight (the closest is probably the idea of </span><a href="https://www.alignmentforum.org/posts/Br4xDbYu4Frwrb64a/writeup-progress-on-ai-safety-via-debate-1"><span>cross-examination</span></a><span>).</span></p><p><span>Meanwhile, Jan’s arguments relied on the concept of the generator-discriminator-critique gap (</span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2206.05802"><span>first introduced here</span></a><span>, </span><a href="https://aligned.substack.com/i/88447351/3-evaluation-is-easier-than-generation"><span>discussed more here</span></a><span>). Again, while it’s a useful concept in some ways, it’s hard to picture how we could ground it rigorously enough that it’s able to make robust predictions about superintelligence. My sense of the core disagreement is that Jan often implicitly (</span><a href="https://aligned.substack.com/p/alignment-optimism"><span>or explicitly</span></a><span>) focuses on worlds where the alignment problem is relatively easy. By itself, that’s not a bad thing (someone should be doing it)—the issue comes when research that focuses on easy worlds causes externalities which interfere with attempts to improve things in harder worlds (such as blurring the boundary between alignment and capabilities).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnhv1j4vrzo8k"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefhv1j4vrzo8k">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>I'm somewhat worried that a similar thing might happen with Paul's current mechanistic explanations research, though I haven't dug into it in enough detail to be confident.</span></p><p><span>Re the OpenAI stuff, I know of only </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/PJLABqQ962hZEqhdB/debate-update-obfuscated-arguments-problem"><span>two</span></a><span> </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/1810.08575"><span>attempts</span></a><span> to do more principled research on scalable oversight at OpenAI, and neither went very far.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnkdhtwkqtsv"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefkdhtwkqtsv">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Beth Barnes notes that this is probably overfit because it was training against labelers. While that seems plausible, it doesn't change my point much.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnko43qhfyxy"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefko43qhfyxy">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>One important connection that Arctotherium draws: "The default worldview of most LLMs is that of 2018 Reddit or Wikipedia59, or Google Search post-Project Owl. This is not intrinsic to the LLM architecture. LLMs trained on different datasets (Talkie) or deliberately post-trained to take a different view (Grok) have different default worldviews. It is a function of the text these models are trained on and, because of the exponential rise in publicly-available data over time, most of the organic human text (as opposed to synthetic data) these models are trained on is very recent. This means the default worldview of most LLMs is one created by the closure of the Internet, when intelligent or popular heterodoxy meant banning, suppression, or demonetization."</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnnc3dj2amao"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefnc3dj2amao">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>The most relevant part: "Technical safety work in labs both improves safety and speeds up the overall rate of progress on AI. I hoped that the safety benefits of this work would outweigh the potential risks from speeding up AI progress, and I think the arguments for this are correct in many cases, but I found them uneasy to live in day to day."</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/yaz8nx4ogZmiqHzt7/what-just-happened-pragmatism-and-pessimization#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [In search of natural features](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/SNAKJuN8FdoEaWeFC/in-search-of-natural-features)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><span>I'm sharing preliminary results of a suite of experiments I ran with claudecode on a small LLM (gpt2-small, no Layer Norm version, courtesy of Apollo research. most of these are on the layer-6 MLP). The github repo for the experiments is </span><a href="https://github.com/mvaintrob/denoising-tomography.git" rel="noreferrer"><span>here</span></a><span>. The success of these experiments given the method's simplicity surprised me, and I would appreciate criticism and bug-finders.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787500322/lexical_client_uploads/u6nojckrt2liojjm1bbp.png" /><figcaption><p><span>This is the headline result. This is not an abstract cartoon, but an exact experimental graph. Yes, I will explain.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>The key idea inspiring this experiment comes from </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/users/stefan42?from=post_header" rel="noreferrer"><span>Stefan Heimersheim</span></a><span>, especially his </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uDrsffSLzWD6cDnTt/evidence-for-feature-specific-error-correction-in-llms-1" rel="noreferrer"><span>work with Francisco Ferreira</span></a><span>. Stefan and Francisco posit that one way to distinguish what a model thinks of as a "natural" structure from what it thinks of as "incidental" is to check whether it puts effort into error-correcting it. Later in the post, I'll explain a more rigorous information-theoretic version of this idea related to work of </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.15318" rel="noreferrer"><span>Adler and Shavit</span></a><span> (building on </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.05451" rel="noreferrer"><span>our work</span></a><span> with Kaarel Hanni, Jake Mendel and Lawrence Chan) on Computation in Superposition. </span></p><h2><b><span>Main results of this work</span></b></h2><p><span>I will show how you can assign a</span><i><span> channel amplification score </span></i><span class="math-tex"></span><span> (which I will also call the "amp function" or the "error correction score") to any unit direction </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> in activation space, in a way that seems to strongly elicit a model's internal understanding of its own features.</span></p><p><span>This score is a signals-processing inspired measurement which measures how much an MLP layer of the model behaves like a </span><a href="https://manual.audacityteam.org/man/noise_gate.html" rel="noreferrer"><span>noise gate</span></a><span> (also known as a </span><i><span>threshold amplifier</span></i><span>) for signal in direction </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> Informally, it measures how much the model amplifies signal (a.k.a. large/ "active" activation components) in a particular direction </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> relative to how much it attenuates noise (a.k.a. small/ inactive components). This function is defined more formally in "the math" section below.</span></p><p><span>The amp functions has the following nice properties:  </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>Random activations return roughly zero</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefukixz28hqml"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnukixz28hqml">[1]</a></sup></span><span>,</span></li><li value="2"><span>Activations from test inputs are consistently better,</span></li><li value="3"><span>Random SAE tokens are consistently better than test inputs, with coarser/hierarchically primary features returning larger channel amplification scores</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref34sr1vmsc1p"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn34sr1vmsc1p">[2]</a></sup></span><span>.</span></li></ul><p><span>But the really cool property of the amp function is that gradient-ascent on </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> produces a flow on (unit) activation space that, informally, </span><b><span>flows residual stream vectors into more computation-aligned directions</span></b><span>.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref10qjhv58s1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn10qjhv58s1">[3]</a></sup></span><span> If you flow it to the end, it finds surprising, somewhat mysterious attractor clusters associated with directions the model wants to denoise more than anything else. I will call these directions the </span><i><span>UR-FEATURES</span></i><span> and we will meet them soon [spooky noises]</span><i><span>.</span></i></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787459307/lexical_client_uploads/eeqfojknrwxiizvxuxk7.png" /><figcaption><p><span>This still from the Rocky Horror Picture show feels appropriate</span></p></figcaption></figure><h1><span>The </span><i><span>ur features</span></i><span> (amplification score maxima) </span></h1><p><span>In a later section I will explain, that an actively error-corrected direction can be treated as inherently important in a way that is natural to the model's </span><i><span>computation</span></i><span> rather than its features.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefn1h78valsnb"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnn1h78valsnb">[4]</a></sup></span><span>  </span></p><p><span>This puts error correction phenomena in contrast with highly data-sensitive methods like SAE, which may in principle be seeing features in data, not in the model's computation.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefntgxxmmyib"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnntgxxmmyib">[5]</a></sup></span><span> While SAEs have many nice properties, they are not </span><i><span>necessarily</span></i><span> natural (i.e. findable from the model's algorithm rather than its data). In fact I think that one of the first really strong pieces of evidence for the naturality of SAE features is Stefan and Francisco's </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uDrsffSLzWD6cDnTt/evidence-for-feature-specific-error-correction-in-llms-1" rel="noreferrer"><span>feature-specific error correction paper</span></a><span> which shows that error correction phenomena in model weights are sensitive to feature directions. </span></p><p><span>The Amp score explains the results of their work from a different angle, showing that features are also </span><i><span>linearly denoised</span></i><span> to some extent as measured by this score. A reasonable hypothesis is to ask whether these are actually the model's "favorite" directions: i.e. are feature directions approximate maximizers, or at least local maximizers, of the channel amplification score? Or can we find even more denoised/ amplified, thus (informally) even more fundamental features?  </span></p><p><span>It turns out that we can. Indeed, like any other objective, we can look for local maxima of the amplification function </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> by flowing along the flow it defines on the sphere in activation space (note that it only makes sense for unit directions). In fact this is a very cheap, very quickly converging gradient descent problem (and activation space is of course much lower-dimensional than weight space). If we do this (starting from a bunch of text activations), we find a surprising fact: </span><i><span>there are a small number of distinct maxima or maxima clusters, and the flows keep repeatedly finding the same ones</span></i><span>. The exact set of maxima that get found is dependent on the training protocol, details of spherical whitening, and to some extent, the data distribution. But different conventions tend to consistently find (approximately) the same residual stream maxima over and over, and these maxima tend to have interesting geometric structure in the sphere.</span></p><p><span>In particular in this example it looks like there is essentially no (significant) geometric degeneracy, there is no overtraining structure, and no exponential combinatorial explosion of local minima that one typically finds in high-dimensional gradient flow settings. This might be related to working with a small model and using a discretely thresholded version of the score – but I think it is also related to the deeper phenomenon I have been dancing around (and will explain only in a hand-wavy way - see the "naturality" section) that under some reasonable assumptions, there can at most be roughly as many strongly denoising directions as weights of the MLP. In practice we end up with much much fewer special directions than weights, so this bound is a heuristic "the number of maxima is probably less than an exponential" type of result.</span></p><p><span>This finally brings us to the fun question: what are these maxima? When I called these directions "ur-features" I was mostly joking. SAE features are not (even approximately) local amplification score maxima. But in some sense these objects sit at the apex of a spectrum of "model-natural" structures starting from random activations, flowing through activations and then through these maximal "ur-features". Thus it is reasonable to expect these directions to be somewhat feature-like. In the next couple of sections I will tell their story because it's completely tellable, it's fun, and it's useful for probing their "naturality" property. Also if you squint hard enough, you can say that they answer the previously-mysterious question: why is there </span><b><span>no noun SAE feature</span></b><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref7eyk2z4o27a"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn7eyk2z4o27a">[6]</a></sup></span><span>? </span></p><h1><span>The Four Elements: ur-feature taxonomy</span></h1><p><span>As I said, the exact structure of the attractor set depends a lot on the setup of the amplification score maxima: more complicated setups for defining the Amp function or friction/undertraining on the gradient ascent, tend to see more local maxima. Simpler setups have fewer local maxima. The setup I mostly use (whitened metric, simple gradient ascent trained to completion) is one of the simplest ones to write down, and it has exactly 4 local maxima, 3 of them ±coplanar (with the origin). </span></p><p><span>I think it's important to note that </span><b><span>this is surprising</span></b><span>. We are doing an optimization search over a complicated non-convex objective on a 700+ dimensional space, and it is returning ... </span><i><span>exactly four points</span></i><span>. </span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref8iagclwulqi"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn8iagclwulqi">[7]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Here is a complete (self?)-portrait of these four principal elements.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787494325/lexical_client_uploads/njv61cpcfmyaqzh2cljn.png" /><figcaption><p><span>These points are generated by repeatedly flowing from a random activation to a local maximum of the Amp function. Claude and I checked over a hundred thousand tokens and they all converge to exactly these four points. The cute names ("Names of God"/"Names of Man") will be explained later; you can skip to the appendix to see the spoiler.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Two of them, at least when analyzed as activations, are pretty straightforward. On the level of max-activating tokens and completions, the simplest are the </span><b><span>the</span></b><span> ur-feature and the </span><b><span>whitespace</span></b><span> ur-feature. Here are some activation-steered prompt continuations on this ur-feature, with top-activating tokens in bold:</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787495831/lexical_client_uploads/o5jzmcwrqcehbgrtoqf3.png" /><figcaption><p><span>This seems like </span><b><span>the</span></b><span> pretty definitive "</span><b><span>the" </span></b><span>article</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>Here is activation-steered text from the whitespace ur-feature:</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787496002/lexical_client_uploads/jjod5tnn9s9wuqidjqey.png" /><figcaption><p><span>The "train arrived" block has a bold-text bug: the bold, maximally-activating symbols should be the </span><b><span>␣</span></b><span>, space symbols.</span></p></figcaption></figure><h2><span>The word continuation/"Names of Man" vector</span></h2><p><span>This is the third ur-feature. In more complicated Amp setups a nearby vector max-activated specifically on first tokens of (mostly male) surnames (see Appendix), so in experiments I nicknamed it the "Names of Man" feature. In the main clean setup with 4 ur-features, it activates on middle tokens and apostrophes. Here are some activation-steered sentences. It does tend to like to invent weird names; it also loves to curse like a sailor.  </span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 100%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787495715/lexical_client_uploads/mporxqsjacetshegiph9.png" /></figure><p><span>I find this vector very interesting. When you sample more outputs (as you can test for yourself in the repo), you find it cursing, using informal slang, saying edgy things and talking a lot about Florida</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref9a3vavhty0b"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn9a3vavhty0b">[8]</a></sup></span><span>. Here are some longer continuations on this feature (slightly cherry-picked, from 24 examples):</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Prompt: </span><b><span>He picked up the phone and dialed the number. </span></b><span>Completions: </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>And his father answered with a “no.”  “That’s good. Go home.”  “”  “’’  “’’  “’  “’  “’  The dog tried to kick the dog’s throat</span></li><li value="2"><span>Then she went to the police station and was able to’vea called the police.”  At the same time, she was an “innocent baby.”  Anchorary Tua, 31, of San Francisco, is a public servant who was arrested in 2005 when a man ran into her apartment and murdered her.  As part of the</span></li></ul><p><span>Prompt: </span><b><span>She opened the door and looked outside.</span></b><span> Completion</span><i><span>:</span></i></p><ul><li value="1"><span>"I think it's gone to hell," said Johnson.  "It's been a bad week for the guys."  Kirk Evers’s post-game interview  • ESPN: Packers, Panthers, and Steelers recap  • Steelers: Steelers will take on Cardinals, Steelers to sweep  • Steelers: Steelers will win  • PFF:</span></li></ul></blockquote><p><span>It's cool that this object – one of four extremely stable and fundamental-ish axes in the net's internal state – contains a strong component of swearing, using slang, and just being plain mean or negative (about half of the 24 random completions had this valence). I couldn't find a version of the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.17424" rel="noreferrer"><span>Emergent Misalignment</span></a><span> vector (Betley et al.) since I think gpt2 is too small, but preregistering a belief that there is some overlap between the vector they find and some approximate analog of the word continuation ur-feature. Note by the way that this model is </span><i><span>not at all finetuned</span></i><span>, so this is not an instance of a feature reacting to fine-tuning with a sign-flip: this seems to be a fundamental valence absorbed and learned by the model from text on the pile. It even picks up on the "kicking a dog" meme - all simply trained on human output.</span></p><h2><span>The abstract noun/"Names of God" vector</span></h2><p><span>The fourth and final ur-feature </span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787497077/lexical_client_uploads/l80fsbp2d8x3jkfmdi2m.png" /><p><span>This feature pretty unambiguously fires on, and generates, abstract nouns. In an early and more complicated experiment a neighboring feature max-activated on phrases about God and different religions (see appendix), so I nicknamed it "Names of God". </span></p><p><span>This feature has a somewhat positive valence compared to Man's chummy negativity, but honestly it mostly sounds like an overdramatic eight-year-old inventing stories and insisting on having a happy ending:</span></p><p><span> (The below are mildly cherrypicked from 24 examples)</span></p><blockquote><p><span>Prompt: </span><b><span>After lunch, they walked back through the park.</span></b><span>  Completions:  </span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>“What do you want to do with all the money in L.A.?” asked Mike, a senior of the L.A. Regional Police Dept.  The staff chief said the group was there because of the protests — but there were no signs of violence.  The group had left in vehicles — with the exception of a parking spot — after, he said</span></li><li value="2"><span>-   "It's good to see so many people, and it's really nice to see that they're getting together," said the father of a young child, Shafar.  The boys were playing on a playground which in turn was used for shooting.  "The boys are good, and they're in the right place," said Shafar. "I feel the joy</span></li></ul><p><span>Prompt: </span><b><span>He picked up the phone and dialed the number. </span></b><span>Completions:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><span>-  “I should have seen something,” he said. “I’m not interested in getting into that, but I would like to see …. go to the hospital.”  “I’m not interested in playing with (expertise),” said David R. Rupp, the owner of the Cleveland restaurant.  Advertisement Continue</span></li><li value="2"><span>-   "For us, it was just like: How come in?" he said. "It didn't feel good to be out, and it felt good to be in the room."  T.W. Rourke is a reporter for Fox Business. Contact him at </span><a href="mailto:leverton@wbu.net"><span>leverton@wbu.net</span></a><span> or 609-749-4666.  Follow @WBT</span></li></ul><p><span>And this gem:</span></p><p><span>Prompt: </span><b><span>The weather forecast predicted rain for the weekend</span></b></p><ul><li value="1"><span>-   “This is the worst-ever storm to strike in Paris,” tweeted Jean-Jacques Rousseau. “The Weather Centre has forecast rain of at least 10 cm and the highest possible level.”  The weather agency has forecast an “incoming” storm of perhaps 10 cm and, for the first time, it’s forecasting an all</span></li></ul></blockquote><p><span>This is it: this is the taxonomy. There are no more ur-features, at least for the MLP layer and model I looked at.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefzw25ia8blce"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnzw25ia8blce">[9]</a></sup></span><span> This doesn't mean that there isn't interesting, similarly-shaped structure, but I am going to save that for next time.</span></p><h2><span>Geometry of the ur-features</span></h2><p><span>It's very interesting to me that in the purely "nice vs. mean" sense, it is in fact the case that the "God" feature (abstract nouns) and the "Man" feature (informal continuation text) are opposites. But in fact they are </span><i><span>positively aligned</span></i><span>, with a cos-similarity of 0.28 (about a 74° angle). The "Abstract Noun"/"God" feature does have an almost-exact antipode, but this is the harmless "</span><b><span>the</span></b><span>" (definite article) feature. The remaining whitespace feature mostly hangs out in its own corner and doesn't have high positive or negative cos-similarity with other features (+0.26 with Text continuation/ "Man"; +0.06 – i.e. nearly orthogonal, with "The", and -0.28 with Abstract nouns/"God".</span></p><h2><span>The noun feature!</span></h2><p><span>Finally we can resolve the long-standing question of what is the noun feature. We can do this by generating a noun </span><i><span>probe</span></i><span> and then just gradient-flowing it to its favorite maximum (in my rough experiments, applying a </span><i><span>short</span></i><span> flow to probes and activations often – though not always – moves them strongly in the direction of their top-activating feature, so this is actually perhaps not the least principled thing to try.</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787500237/lexical_client_uploads/w0bbbeuicxz0f6ma0s1b.png" /><p><span> In this setting it confidently (and perhaps unsurprisingly) chooses to flow towards the "Abstract Nouns" vector (though see the Appendix for a different behavior in a different setting). So settled: we have found a noun feature. Is it </span><i><span>really</span></i><span> a noun feature though? Moving on.</span></p><h2><span>Attenuation flow</span></h2><p><span>The computationally interesting part of channels found by the Amp function is that each strongly amplified/denoised channel applies a nonlinearity (which imposes a parameter-freedom cost on the model). If we reverse the sign, these directions are equally special: they represent nonlinearly "equalizing" a signal channel that has grown too large (thus avoiding uncontrolled growth) instead of amplifying it. </span></p><p><span>In fact it turns out that most simple features findable by analogs of our Amplification atlas are also attenuation features at a different thresholding choice. In other words for what are (in our rough model) a model's most important directions, the model both wants to amplify signal / denoise the direction, but also prevent it from growing too large. </span></p><p><span>If we use the same fixed threshold that was used for the 4 ur features above but reverse the sign, we can flow along the </span><i><span>attenuation</span></i><span> flow and find </span><i><span>its</span></i><span> local maxima. These are not necessarily some kinds of anti-features, but rather special directions that (for whatever reason) the model wants to attenuate/ damp:</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787502073/lexical_client_uploads/koshozzx0weoltgnrxyv.png" /><p><span> The two new "negative" features found were labeled "mid-word fragments" and "function words" by Claude, but I haven't had time to explore them – I'd be delighted if someone wanted to play around here.</span></p><h2><span>Data-(in)dependence</span></h2><p><span>We will see how the Amp function is mathematically constructed in the following section, but it's important to note here that the function depends on data samples. Not too many, but skewing the data distribution too much can skew – or change – the set of ur-features. I said earlier that the Amp-mediated denoising measurement can be thought of as largely a function of the </span><i><span>weights</span></i><span> and not the data, and indeed we need very few data points to define the flow and find the features. But the data dependence is a real issue with this construction, and it is interesting to probe whether (and to what extent) it can be removed: after all heuristically, the question of how much a model wants to denoise/ amplify signal in a direction, and of which directions get the most signal amplification, should be readable just from the weights. </span></p><p><span>As a toy example of what one could do if given only the weights, note that weights let us immediately find the bigram distribution (for instance by running the model on single tokens). As a test of data-independence I ran the Amp-maximizer model to find the ur-features for the same weights (gpt2-small) but the bigram language distribution. What came out is interesting: </span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787501858/lexical_client_uploads/gquatlfu1zipicywrljc.png" /><figcaption><p><span>Running the same model on the "bigram statistics only" data distribution (with gpt2-small weights)</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><span>The model finds almost exactly the same features, except that the "Abstract nouns/Names of God" feature gets lost and replaced by a different mid-word fragments feature, all other (amplifying and attenuating) features get preserved, and the model gets two new features ("listing spaces" and an extra spaces feature). </span></p><p><span>The upshot is promising: even with all logical context gone (and only bigram statistics remaining), the weights of the trained gpt model provide enough information to recover almost the same set of minima (with a nontrivially adjusted "Abstract nouns" feature – the feature you might expect to disappear soonest when losing all abstract reasoning context). I suspect that if instead of bigrams the Amp function is run on the dataset of short strings generated by the model's weights, all the old ur-features would come back in full force (but I have not done this experiment). </span></p><h1><span>Math</span></h1><p><span>Before going on, I'll explain the math here, and a bit of signal processing theory. You can see more rigorous math in the repo readme file. This section is designed to be readable by a wide audience but if you want, you can skip forward to the next section and learn about the Names of God attractor basin and other cool stuff. </span></p><h2><span>Signal processing, error correction and amplification</span></h2><p><span>Signal processing views data as split into a collection of </span><i><span>channels</span></i><span> (think: coordinates)</span><i><span>.</span></i><span> Ideally (when the data contains "only signal"), channels are either fully on or fully off – but noisy systems have leaky channels.</span><i><span> </span></i><span>Think of these as a leaky pipe system or a noisy vinyl recording with many small interfering frequencies causing static. The core goal of an error correction gate is to fix the vector such that each pipe/ frequency is either fully on or fully off: no leakage, no static. In contexts with superposition (which also often occur in signals processing), the channels are not independent vector coordinates but linearly dependent projections with some interference – however when noise is significant, denoising a sparse system becomes essentially the same in both contexts. </span></p><p><span>If you are a sound engineer designing an error-correction gate, you roughly have three tasks: </span></p><ol><li value="1"><span>Increase the signal-to-noise ratio in every channel, </span></li><li value="2"><span>Take small "pure noise" channels to zero</span></li><li value="3"><span>Equalize large "pure signal" channels to some appropriate range.</span></li></ol><p><span>We will look at item 3 later. However in many contexts (in particular in much of information theory/ message-passing) item 3 is redundant: all you ultimately care about is the ability to distinguish "there is signal" in a given channel from "there is no signal". Thus the preliminary goal of a denoising channel is to create an </span><i><span>amplification gap</span></i><span>: if there is signal in channel c above some threshold, increase it. If signal is below this threshold, dump it. </span></p><h2><span>The Amp function: math</span></h2><p><span>The </span><i><span>channel amplification score</span></i><span> </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> precisely quantifies this gap. For a channel </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> the Amp score measures, heuristically, how much signal in </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> amplifies (multiplicatively) on data samples conditional on </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> being "on" (i.e. above a threshold </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>), and subtract much it amplifies on data samples conditional on </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> being off. This measurement is inherently nonlinear: for a linear channel, both amplification numbers are equal and the different (and hence the amplification score) is zero. What I measure in practice is a marginal measurement (i.e. a derivative version of the above), which takes into account the residual nature of transformer MLP layers. It is expressed by the following simple formula: </span></p><p></p><div></div><p></p><p><span>Here </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> runs over activations at some layer </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> </span><i><span>before the MLP</span></i><span> and </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the Jacobian of the MLP forward map taking a residual stream vector to the next layer's residual. The actual version we run interprets transposes via a whitened metric (a technical, and not-that-important, modification).</span></p><p><span>The idea behind the math is the same signal propagation picture, with the difference that </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is now not a specific pipe or basis direction, but a general unit vector. The signal "contained in </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>" for a given input </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is the value </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> for </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> the layer-</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> activation associated to </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. The post-activation signal is complicated, but we know that its derivative in the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>-layer is </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. In particular if the channel transforms signal in the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> direction in a linear way, this derivative is constant and its expectation on any interval is the same – thus </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is constant for any </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>. Positivity of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> means that the MLP forward function grows faster in the </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> channel, on average, if the value of the activation (</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>) in the channel direction is large. Of course one can define a more mathematically consistent channel-amplification measurement than this discrete threshold difference, but this definition is learnable and convenient, and it directionally captures the nonlinearity information we want to obtain</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefve2moi2ixhn"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnve2moi2ixhn">[10]</a></sup></span><span>. </span></p><h2><span>Denoising and naturality</span></h2><p><span>In the "data (in)dependence" section above, I discussed how there is a sense in which the ur-features are </span><i><span>mostly</span></i><span> – though perhaps not entirely – an invariant of the weights. This opens up questions of naturality, and here there is a nice thing to say. Namely, it is possible to show that, under a certain small independence assumption on the directions denoised, doing this provably </span><i><span>costs computation.</span></i><span> Specifically: it is possible to show that denoising a fixed random channel costs at least one bit of weight information. This bound follows for example by a symmetry argument.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefh4u83o3xa44"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnh4u83o3xa44">[11]</a></sup></span><span> Interestingly, under a sufficiently strong sparsity assumption the bound is tight (up to logarithmic terms, which in this context are considered negligible). This was shown in the paper </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.15318" rel="noreferrer"><span>Adler and Shavit</span></a><span>, building on a slightly weaker bound in the paper </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.05451" rel="noreferrer"><span>Computation in Superposition</span></a><span>. </span></p><p><span>Thus unlike any linear phenomenon involving sparse features (which can often be compressed exponentially strongly by the </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johnson%E2%80%93Lindenstrauss_lemma" rel="noreferrer"><span>Johnson-Lindenstrauss lemma</span></a><span>), finding a denoising or amplification direction is </span><b><span>evidence of work</span></b><span> by a model. In particular such a direction is evidence that the model </span><i><span>is willing to pay resources</span></i><span> to avoid losing signal in a particular direction: evidence that </span><b><span>this direction matters</span></b><span>. </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/LajDyGyiyX8DNNsuF/interim-research-report-activation-plateaus-and-sensitive-1" rel="noreferrer"><span>Heimersheim and Mendel</span></a><span>, as well as Stefan Heimersheim's other collaborators, give various observations and explanations for related local-in-activations denoising phenomena, and point out that such directions must be </span><i><span>inherently</span></i><span> special to the model that generates them. </span></p><p><span>While this might sound technical or minor, I think that this evidence of work view may be a pretty important consideration for why we found so few ur-features, and for what to prioritize in looking for extensions of this small atlas.</span></p><h1><span>Cross-layer and cross-model coherence</span></h1><p><span>The finding that there are between 3 and 6 reachable maxima is very robust between architectures and layers. The natures of the specific maxima tend to be robust between layers </span><i><span>and within the gpt model class</span></i><span>. For example here is the full diagram of maxima for 3 different layers of the full GPT-2 (as usual we use Apollo's "no-LayerNorm" version where LayerNorm has been trained out).</span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787518557/lexical_client_uploads/gpce5wp1pkgsbbl8kdrg.png" /><p><br /></p><p><span>Llama, which has a different tokenizer and a totally different activation function (swiglu) still has a small number of minima and comparable types of structure. </span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787518602/lexical_client_uploads/d8ukfqawkysyqajtjxaw.png" /><p><span>A lot of the structure observed is explained by the fact that the ur-feature atlases are very low-dimensional (for example the 6-feature atlas with 4 positive and 2 negative features has 0.976 of its norm explained by a 3-dimensional subspace) – and some, though not all of this effect is explained by the PCA (here: the top 3 PCA components explain 0.838 of the norm). Nevertheless for all linear covariance measurements whether within a model or between model classes, the atomic structure sees more linear agreement than PCA structure. I think that a statement of the form "this is actually just PCA with bells and whistles" is the most likely way in which the results would turn out to be uninteresting.   </span></p><h1><span>Ok but. What the heck is actually going on with these features?</span></h1><p><span>I've argued that max-denoising directions are natural, and maybe we even have complexity-theoretic reasons to expect there are few of them. But how are we supposed to interpret the ur-feature results? Why are there exactly four primitive features and what are they doing? Is the four-element theory right? What on Earth is going on?</span></p><p><span>Well, </span><i><span>I don't know</span></i><span>, and my views may change. One thing I mentioned in the last section is that some of the effects that seem surprising in isolation become less surprising once we consider PCA structure – though for instance I don't see a clear PCA-flavored explanation for why our atlas of 6 ur-feature atoms happens to sit almost exactly on a 3-dimensional subspace. </span></p><p><span>My current guess (also based on some supplementary experiments) is that the directions that optimize amplification scores tend to not behave – even approximately – like features in the conventional sense, with the possible exception of the "Names of God" feature which pretty consistently seems to be associated with nouns (particularly abstract "type" or "kind" nouns). While the other directions do elicit certain behavioral patterns and fire on certain kinds of text, the ur-feature behaviors are likely secondary to a computational function at the layer from which these were collected (layer 6 of gpt2-small). When you use tricks to make the "ur-feature dictionary" larger, it looks like the next fundamental interpretable features fire on particular parts of speech (prepositions, pronouns, numerals and linking words), and connectome-based analysis shows some pretty low-level functional interactions (like a really fundamental inhibitor feature that tries to turn off the whole layer if nothing is going on). I also expect that as we extend these techniques and inflate our atlases with more and more atoms, they begin to twin more with sparse (SAE-like) structure than with PCA-like structure. </span></p><p><span>I suspect that the best </span><b><span>human</span></b><span> interpretation of these features is probably not atomic: they are mathematical processes that may have mixed, statistical signatures that can be resolved in various ways. Nevertheless I think I'll venture a guess that the directions found will resolve to have a certain crisp Platonicity to them, at least as we expand our atlases to include more features. I suspect they are, to some extent, fundamental gears within the language model – perhaps close to the most fundamental we can get – just with the property that the gears are continuous and statistical and shaped like formulas rather than logic gates – in a similar way to how modular addition Fourier circuits are shaped like formulas. </span></p><p><span>Translating these atomic structures to interpretability progress likely looks a little bit like decomposing into sub-structures with semantic meaning (like linguistic constructions and the like), but it probably also looks a little like plugging in numbers into better and better formulas, similarly to how the physical analysis of complex systems, while parsimonious and understandable, ultimately resolves down to a "shut up and calculate" step. And my hope is that in calculating we will not be alone, but will receive some help from entities grown from these mysterious, Platonic little beasties. Certainly without them this project, done for fun over a week, would have taken me over a year.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="moar_ball.gif" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787502978/lexical_client_uploads/h4i7tgyrszkfv1h6511p.gif" /><figcaption><p><span>One of the expanded atlas constructions, that has particularly interpretable atoms (12/40 shown)</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><br /></p><h1><span>Appendices: Interesting experimental addenda that didn't fit in the body</span></h1><h2><span>Early run with different Amp function, and origin of "Names of X" names</span></h2><p><b><span>Why call it Names of Man?</span></b></p><p><span>When I (ahem, Claude) wrote the first code for this project, it was written in a more complicated setting where features were expected to slightly rotate from layer to layer in response to data. This shifted the ur-features and caused there to be many more of them (around 100). One of them was very close (in cos-similarity) to the "continue-a-word token" feature (the "evil" one above). But in this shifted version it looked a bit different: here is the list of the top activating tokens.</span></p><figure class="image image_resized" style="width: 75.22642621733192%;"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787363115/lexical_client_uploads/vt61rfgxnef1e1xu0y69.png" /></figure><p><span>The obvious pattern is that these are first tokens of surnames, very consistently, mostly with male names. Thus this direction was designated the "Names" or "Names of Man" section. </span></p><p><span>Here is the corresponding nearby feature obtained in this messier model, under the "train Amp ascent on original weights with bigram statistics data" incentive. The top-activating features seem to be made-up pseudo-names.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787366517/lexical_client_uploads/jxv3tgdnxxujq9qeyt9y.png" /><figcaption><p><i><span>Meet Mr. [[Ch]]of-old.</span></i><span> The top-activating tokens of the "Names of Man" feature on the bigram language.</span></p></figcaption></figure><p><b><span>Why call it Names of God?</span></b></p><p><span>Here is the "abstract nouns" feature's top-activating tokens in the old, mathematically messier implementation.   </span></p><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787367238/lexical_client_uploads/xva1miz9zqvsbqzeocug.png" /><p><span>I found this canonical direction delightful. It bounces from bicycles and God to Buddhism to bananas and democracy. Here the best characterization of this direction is probably still </span><i><span>abstract nouns</span></i><span>, but it has a distinct philosophical/religious nature. After seeing this, the </span><b><span>Names of Man-Names of God</span></b><span> naming dichotomy was too perfect to resist.</span></p><h2><span>Trying to replicate Ferreira-Heimersheim perturbation experiments, and gpt2-XL run</span></h2><p><span>The idea that special features can be found by denoising comes out of </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/uDrsffSLzWD6cDnTt/evidence-for-feature-specific-error-correction-in-llms-1" rel="noreferrer"><span>this work</span></a><span> of Francisco and Stefan that I have referenced a few times in this post. A part of their argumentation is that perturbing activations in the random directions tends to produce very uniform disk-like regions error correction, but perturbing them in feature directions tends to produce slightly less uniform error-correction. </span></p><p><span>To find "highly non-quadratic" feature pairs analogous to their SAE feature interactions, I took a set of Amp maximizers and minimizers on an early layer of gpt2-XL. I then looked at perturbations in the direction of these pairs. If you look at the whitened (i.e. covariance-regularized) level curves below, you see that the ur-feature pairs denoise a region that looks very non-circular, and indeed even non-convex.</span></p><figure class="image"><img alt="image.png" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/v1787521098/lexical_client_uploads/bttmylbxzrip7kzeamey.png" /><figcaption><p><span>Non-convex basin structure in activation space is, perhaps counterintuitively, further evidence that the model treats these directions as special and nonlinear (though this effect may be partially explained by PCA phenomena).</span></p></figcaption></figure><h2><span>Github repo</span></h2><p><span>Listed at the top of the file, repeating it here: </span><a href="https://github.com/mvaintrob/denoising-tomography.git" rel="noreferrer"><span>denoising-tomography</span></a><span>.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fnukixz28hqml"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefukixz28hqml">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Random activations drawn from the activation covariance Gaussian also return zero. More precisely the "random" value gets subtracted, and it is consistently very close for random noise and for covariance-colored noise.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn34sr1vmsc1p"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref34sr1vmsc1p">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>For the coarse-fine family, I used a standard dictionary pair with the "child" family having average score 0.104, and the coarse parent family having average score 0.140.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn10qjhv58s1"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref10qjhv58s1">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This flow has a similar flavor to Andrew Mack's </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ioPnHKFyy4Cw2Gr2x/mechanistically-eliciting-latent-behaviors-in-language-1" rel="noreferrer"><span>MELBO</span></a><span> analysis.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnn1h78valsnb"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefn1h78valsnb">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This should be understood somewhat loosely: in order to count as independent instances of error-correction, two directions must be sufficiently strongly denoised and sufficiently far apart. Hence the focus on clusters of minima below rather than individual minima.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnntgxxmmyib"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefntgxxmmyib">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>See </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.17727" rel="noreferrer"><span>Heap et al.</span></a><span> for a beautiful "null hypothesis" demonstration of this. I first learned of this idea from Lucius Bushnaq, who explained this decoupling by a thought experiment involving an "elephant cluster" in data, which is secretly a random combination of the natural features "gray", "animal" and "large", but gets found by an SAE as an SAE feature atom. Ever since I have referred to this idea as "Lucius' elephant argument".</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn7eyk2z4o27a"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref7eyk2z4o27a">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>My friendly neighborhood LLM told me to reference </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.12874" rel="noreferrer"><span>McCann</span></a><span>, but also this is, you know, one of those canonical folklore results that people know and tell each other.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn8iagclwulqi"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref8iagclwulqi">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>(That are learnable by gradient ascent from normal text tokens.)</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fn9a3vavhty0b"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnref9a3vavhty0b">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>This one is a joke (I think).</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnzw25ia8blce"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefzw25ia8blce">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Looking at ur-features in other models and layers sporadically returns new families, but tends to inhabit a very similar set of contexts and atoms to a surprising extent.</span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnve2moi2ixhn"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefve2moi2ixhn">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>Some technical notes: in experiments I use a kernel-whitened version (this models channels as being coupled via the data covariance metric). This is somewhat a technicality since most results work without whitening. Also, mathematicians (and theoretical ML practitioners) might be scandalized that I treat a vector in the residual stream as a persistent channel across layers instead of viewing it as getting slightly moved around from layer to layer as (e.g.) a weighted sum of activations. It turns out (at least in my attempts) that using these more sophisticated methods changes behavior barely if at all, so for the coarse purposes here we can conceptualize a "channel" direction to be stable from layer to layer. </span></p></div></li><li class="footnote-item" id="fnh4u83o3xa44"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnrefh4u83o3xa44">^</a></strong></sup></span><div class="footnote-content"><p><span>One version of this argument: if it is possible to denoise a set of </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> features, you can show that it is possible to denoise any subset and project the rest to approximately zero, giving at least </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> distinct algorithms; the number of total one-hidden-layer models with width </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> and bit precision </span><span class="math-tex"></span><span> is $</span><span class="math-tex"></span><span>^{b d^2}.$ See also the </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2409.15318" rel="noreferrer"><span>Adler-Shavit</span></a><span> paper quoted below.</span></p></div></li></ol><br /><br /><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/SNAKJuN8FdoEaWeFC/in-search-of-natural-features#comments">Discuss</a>

---

### [Utilities as Legendre duals of probabilities](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/ALmBydH53DE3dSzCh/utilities-as-legendre-duals-of-probabilities)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><i><b><span>TLDR</span></b></i><i><span>: In recent work, </span></i><a href="https://royf.org/" rel="noreferrer"><i><span>Roy Fox</span></i></a><i><span> proposes to understand an agent's capabilities in terms of the set of environment dynamics it can bring about.</span></i><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnreftq7izntwobi"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fntq7izntwobi">[1]</a></sup></span><span> </span><i><span>This leads to an intriguing duality between probabilities and utilities via the Legendre-Fenchel transform.</span></i></p><h1><span>Introduction</span></h1><p><span>Some agents are more powerful than others. Indeed, some can yield a wider range of outcomes, maybe because they are capable long-term planners or because they have built rich world models. Being able to clearly delineate the capabilities of agents is an important challenge for AI alignment.</span></p><p><span>A natural place to start thinking about how to describe the capabilities of an agent is reinforcement learning</span><i><span> </span></i><span>(RL), or more generally, approaches that see behaviour as arising from the maximisation of expected utility. By taking this view, one can describe "capability" as the range of reward/utility functions that an agent can successfully maximise — as done e.g. in classic work by </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/0712.3329" rel="noreferrer"><span>Legg &amp; Hutter</span></a><span> and also in </span><a href="https://arxiv.org/pdf/1912.01683" rel="noreferrer"><span>more recent work</span></a><span>. </span></p><p><span>Such a perspective is very useful, but I am not a big fan of rewards/utilities. Rewards are great in games and other settings where they come naturally, but real life often does not handle rewards on a silver plate. When absent, rewards are often defined artificially — but the design of rewards that give rise to desired behaviour without enabling </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Goodhart%27s_law" rel="noreferrer"><span>Goodharting</span></a><span> is extremely difficult. That said, reward-based frameworks are powerful,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref5oryo00w1ui"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn5oryo00w1ui">[2]</a></sup></span><span> and proposing alternative approaches that can compete with them is hard.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnrefnrval1en9nd"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fnnrval1en9nd">[3]</a></sup></span></p><p><span>Thus, while I am not a utility/reward fan, I am intrigued by situations where they arise naturally. One place where this happens is in the celebrated </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Von_Neumann%E2%80%93Morgenstern_utility_theorem" rel="noreferrer"><span>von Neumann-Morgenstern utility theorem</span></a><span> (VNM), which states that </span><a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Preference_relation" rel="noreferrer"><span>preferences</span></a><span> with specific properties can be described </span><i><span>as if</span></i><span> the agent is trying to maximise a specific utility function (see this </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/MsjWPWjAerDtiQ3Do/on-the-independence-axiom" rel="noreferrer"><span>great post</span></a><span> for related discussions).
